Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 65 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

A function or option has changed since you wrote the code, so it stops with today’s version of the package.

  1. In R, run renv::init() in your project and add the renv.lock file this creates to the deposit; in Python, run pip freeze > requirements.txt and add that file.
  2. State your version of R or Python in the README.
  3. Better still, update the code so it also works with current versions.

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

In Phase 4, readers were randomized to the reference sheet alone, AI predictions, or predictions with gradient-weighted class activation mapping (Grad-CAM).

At Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital, participants with Takotsubo syndrome, AMI, and healthy volunteers were assigned at the participant level to the training, validation, and pretraining-validation datasets in an approximate ratio of 75%, 20%, and 5%, respectively, such that each participant was included in only one dataset.

In Phase 4, participants were assigned in a 1:1:1 ratio using stratified block randomization by cardiologist status and years of clinical experience to reference sheet alone, reference sheet plus AI predictions without Grad-CAM, or reference sheet plus AI predictions with Grad-CAM.

Seventy participants completed the reader study, including 36 cardiologists (51%); participants’ characteristics were balanced across randomized groups (Supplementary Table S5).

In Phases 1 -3, the GLMM-estimated probability of correct diagnosis was 0.632 (95% CI, In the randomized Phase 4, 22, 23, and 25 participants were assigned to the reference sheet-only, AI-without-Grad-CAM, and AI-with-Grad-CAM groups, respectively.

Readers were randomized to the reference sheet alone (n = 22), the reference sheet plus AI predictions without Grad-CAM (n = 23), or the reference sheet plus AI predictions with Grad-CAM (n = 25).

Ethics approval statement:

This study was approved by the Institutional Review Board of Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital, which waived informed consent for patients, healthy volunteers, and readers, and was conducted in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki.

The Institutional Review Board of Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital approved this study and waived the requirement for informed consent from patients, healthy volunteers, and readers (08-1-42).

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 26 columns across 4 data files.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 4 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Background and Aims: Artificial intelligence (AI) may help differentiate Takotsubo syndrome from acute anterior ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI) on electrocardiograms (ECGs). We developed an ECG image-based model and evaluated whether AI assistance and visual explanations improved reader performance, overall and by reader background.

Methods: A self-supervised three-architecture deep learning ensemble was developed and externally validated using 12-lead ECG images from two institutions, recorded ≤14 days after onset regardless of reperfusion. A four-phase reader study compared unaided interpretation, a reference sheet, and AI assistance (Phases 1-3). In Phase 4, readers were randomized to the reference sheet alone, AI predictions, or predictions with gradient-weighted class activation mapping (Grad-CAM). Correctness was analyzed with generalized linear mixed-effects models.

Results: Cohorts comprised 832 development and 95 external validation participants.

External validation accuracy was 0.914, and the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve was 0.970 (95% CI, 0.938-0.992), or 0.958 using first recorded ECGs. Among 70 readers, the estimated probability of correct diagnosis increased from 0.632 unaided to 0.864 with AI (odds ratio [OR], 3.71; P<0.001). In Phase 4, AI assistance improved accuracy vs reference sheet (OR, 4.28; 95% CI, 3.08-5.95), and Grad-CAM added value (OR, 1.53; 95% CI, 1.04-2.25). In exploratory analyses, less experienced readers gained more; with AI, non-cardiologists outperformed cardiologists. Mean AI-assisted reader accuracy remained below model accuracy.

Conclusions: An ECG image-based ensemble differentiated Takotsubo syndrome from anterior STEMI and improved reader performance, particularly with visual explanations. Experience-related differences emphasize the need for effective human-AI interaction.

Introduction

Takotsubo syndrome, also known as stress cardiomyopathy and initially termed "Takotsubo cardiomyopathy" in Hiroshima, often presents with chest pain, electrocardiographic changes, and left ventricular wall-motion abnormalities that closely resemble acute myocardial infarction (AMI). [1] [2] [3] [4] In ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI), prompt reperfusion therapy is essential for reducing mortality, whereas Takotsubo syndrome does not require similarly time-critical disease-specific treatment. Therefore, early differentiation between these conditions represents an important clinical challenge. Although invasive coronary angiography is often required to exclude obstructive coronary artery disease, patients with Takotsubo syndrome may present with conditions that increase procedural risk. Therefore, accurate identification of true Takotsubo syndrome may support consideration of noninvasive alternatives and help avoid unnecessary invasive coronary angiography in selected patients. Electrocardiography (ECG) is a rapid, noninvasive test routinely used in the initial evaluation of chest pain and is recommended within 10 minutes of first medical contact. 5 Several studies have proposed ECG-based criteria to distinguish Takotsubo syndrome from AMI; however, their diagnostic performance remains suboptimal in real-world practice. This limitation is partly due to variability in clinical presentation, including spontaneous recanalization in AMI and temporal changes in ECG waveforms in both conditions, which can obscure distinguishing features.

Transthoracic echocardiography is also widely used and may aid in differentiation based on characteristic wall-motion patterns. However, accurate differentiation can remain challenging because of suboptimal imaging conditions in emergency settings. Consequently, Takotsubo syndrome is often diagnosed only after coronary angiography has been performed for suspected AMI.

Recent advances in artificial intelligence (AI), particularly deep learning, have enabled automated ECG analysis, with several studies demonstrating high diagnostic performance across various cardiovascular tasks. 6, 7 However, the clinical utility of such models depends not only on their predictive performance but also on their integration into clinical workflows and their potential to support human decision-making. 8 The aims of this study were threefold: (1) to develop and externally validate an AI model for differentiating Takotsubo syndrome from acute anterior STEMI using ECGs obtained within 14 days after symptom onset, regardless of reperfusion status, and compare its performance with that of human readers; (2) to assess whether AI assistance improves reader diagnostic performance and whether visual explanations provide additional benefit; and (3) to explore whether performance gains and accuracy with AI assistance vary by cardiologist status and clinical experience, extending our previous work on an AI utilization gap. 8

Methods

Study patients and data collection

This study included three subsets of participants: patients with acute anterior STEMI, patients with Takotsubo syndrome, and healthy volunteers. The three subsets were retrospectively identified at Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital and Asa Medical Association Hospital. Patients diagnosed with acute anterior STEMI with a culprit lesion in the left-main trunk or left anterior descending artery (LAD), or with Takotsubo syndrome, between 1996 and 2024 were eligible for inclusion. The diagnosis of Takotsubo syndrome was based mainly on the Mayo criteria or the InterTAK Diagnostic Criteria, and patients with intracranial hemorrhage or brain trauma were also eligible for inclusion. 9, 10 Among eligible patients with Takotsubo syndrome, those who underwent coronary angiography or computed tomography were included in this study. Among eligible STEMI patients, 321 and 40 patients from Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital and Asa Medical Association Hospital, respectively, were randomly sampled and included, based on a preliminary study indicating that these sample sizes would yield numbers of ECG images approximately balanced with those obtained if all candidate patients with Takotsubo syndrome were included. We reviewed the medical records of the study patients and collected ECGs. ECGs recorded within 14 days after symptom onset, with or without coronary revascularization, were labeled "Takotsubo" or "AMI," whereas all ECGs recorded before symptom onset or at least 1 year after onset were labeled "non-takotsubo/AMI." ECGs recorded between 14 days and 1 year after symptom onset were excluded. Healthy staff volunteers with ECGs recorded during routine health examinations and no relevant medical history were also included. For healthy volunteers, all recorded ECGs were collected and labeled "non-takotsubo/AMI." Twelve-lead ECG images were obtained by electronic export or photography of paper ECGs or screen displays; acquisition details are provided in the Supplementary Methods.

At Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital, participants with Takotsubo syndrome, AMI, and healthy volunteers were assigned at the participant level to the training, validation, and pretraining-validation datasets in an approximate ratio of 75%, 20%, and 5%, respectively, such that each participant was included in only one dataset. An external test dataset was constructed from data collected at Asa Medical Association Hospital. Data from the former hospital were used for model development, whereas data from the latter hospital were used for external evaluation of the models and for the human diagnostic test. This study was approved by the Institutional Review Board of Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital, which waived informed consent for patients, healthy volunteers, and readers, and was conducted in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki.

Pretraining datasets

For self-supervised pretraining, 1,754,276 ECG images were generated from the publicly available MIMIC-IV-ECG, A Large Scale 12-Lead Electrocardiogram Database for Arrhythmia Study, PTB-XL, and Georgia 12-Lead ECG Challenge Dataset. [11] [12] [13] [14] Our institutional dataset was also used for pretraining. Additional details are provided in the Supplementary Methods.

Model development

We developed an ECG image-based deep learning ensemble model to distinguish Takotsubo syndrome from anterior STEMI with a culprit lesion in the left main trunk or LAD, with or without coronary reperfusion. Three backbone models-ViT, ConvNeXtV2, and MLP-Mixer-were pretrained using masked autoencoder-based self-supervised learning and subsequently fine-tuned, first for 3-class classification (Takotsubo syndrome, AMI, and non-Takotsubo/AMI) and then for binary classification (Takotsubo syndrome vs AMI). During inference, test-time augmentation (TTA) was applied where appropriate. The final ensemble prediction was generated by averaging logits from the three component models with equal weights. 15 Details of the model architectures, pretraining, fine-tuning, checkpoint selection, and TTA are provided in the Supplementary Methods.

Evaluation of models with external dataset

A predicted probability of ≥ 0.5 was used to classify an ECG as AMI. Performance metrics were assessed using accuracy, precision, sensitivity (recall), specificity, F1 score, expected calibration error (ECE), Brier score, and Brier skill score (BSS). Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) and precision-recall (PR) curves were constructed using the predicted probability of AMI, and the corresponding areas under the curves (AUCs) were calculated. The external dataset was used for evaluation only, without model recalibration or retraining. To approximate the initial diagnostic decision before coronary angiography, performance was also evaluated using images derived from each patient's first recorded ECG (before reperfusion in AMI). Three same-architecture ensembles, each comprising three models, were compared with the final ensemble in an exploratory analysis (Supplementary Methods). Because multiple images were derived from each patient, 95% confidence intervals (CIs) were estimated using 5,000 patient-cluster bootstrap replicates stratified by diagnosis, and a sensitivity analysis weighted each image by the reciprocal of the patient's number of images (Supplementary Methods).

Human diagnostic test

In a four-phase reader study, volunteer healthcare providers with varying ECG experience interpreted ECGs without additional clinical information. Two 50-image ECG sets were randomly sampled from the test set: one for Phases 1 -3 and another for Phase 4. Phases 1 -3 involved unaided interpretation, a criteria-based reference sheet derived from prior reports [16] [17] [18] [19] , and the reference sheet plus AI assistance, respectively (Supplementary Figure S1). AI assistance in Phase 3 included predictions from the three component models and their ensemble, and gradient-weighted class activation mapping (Grad-CAM) visualizations from ViT and ConvNeXtV2. In Phase 4, participants were assigned in a 1:1:1 ratio using stratified block randomization by cardiologist status and years of clinical experience to reference sheet alone, reference sheet plus AI predictions without Grad-CAM, or reference sheet plus AI predictions with Grad-CAM. For each ECG, readers provided a binary diagnosis (Takotsubo syndrome vs AMI). Accuracy, sensitivity, and specificity for AMI were calculated. Set composition and randomization details are provided in the Supplementary Methods.

Statistical analysis

Continuous variables are presented as medians with first and third quartiles or as mean ± standard deviation, as appropriate. Categorical variables are presented as numbers and percentages. No missing data were observed in the baseline characteristics of the study patients. values were adjusted using the Holm method, and 95% CIs for the odds ratios (ORs) were adjusted using the Bonferroni method. In Phases 1 -3, separate exploratory GLMMs assessed interactions of phase with cardiologist status or clinical experience. In Phase 4, the primary comparison was reference sheet alone vs both AI-assisted groups combined; the secondary comparison was AI assistance with vs without Grad-CAM. Sensitivity analyses adjusted both the Phase 4 comparisons for cardiologist status and experience (< 10 vs ≥ 10 years). Accuracy was the proportion of correct diagnoses, with AMI as the positive class for sensitivity and specificity. Secondary participant-level analyses are described in the Supplementary Methods.

Sample size estimation for the human diagnostic study was based on participant-level accuracy in the pilot data. Because the comparison between AI assistance with and without Grad-CAM required the largest sample size, the target sample size was set at 20 participants per group.

With 1:1:1 randomization in Phase 4, the planned total sample size was 60 participants.

Additional details are provided in the Supplementary Methods.

We utilized Python 3.12 (Python Software Foundation, Delaware, USA) for model development, human test code, and statistical analyses. GLMMs were fitted using R software version 4.6 (R Foundation for Statistical Computing, Vienna, Austria) with the lme4 package. 20 The TRIPOD+AI and STARD-AI reporting checklists are provided in the Supplementary Material.

Results

Baseline characteristics

Figure 1 summarizes the study. The development dataset comprised 832 participants (308 with Takotsubo syndrome, 321 with AMI, and 203 healthy controls) and 68,805 ECG images. The external validation dataset comprised 95 participants and 13,132 ECG images (Table 1). All patients with AMI underwent immediate coronary angiography, and primary percutaneous coronary intervention was attempted in 99%. Development-set partition sizes are shown in Supplementary Table S8.

External validation performance of the AI models

Representative ECG images with corresponding model predictions and visualization for Takotsubo syndrome and AMI are shown in Supplementary Figure S2. The final ensemble achieved an accuracy of 0.914, sensitivity of 0.950, specificity of 0.846, and ROC AUC of 0.970; additional performance metrics are shown in Table 2, Figure 1, and Figure 2.

External ablation analyses showed improved performance with ImageNet initialization and the highest performance with self-supervised pretraining, compared with training from scratch.

TTA further improved ViT and MLP-Mixer performance; it was not used for ConvNeXtV2 (Supplementary Table S1). The final ensemble had the highest observed ROC AUC (0.970 vs 0.952, 0.965, and 0.959 for the ViT-only, ConvNeXtV2-only, and MLP-Mixer only ensembles, respectively; Supplementary Table S2).

Using images derived from each patient's first recorded ECG in the external validation datasetbefore reperfusion in AMI and after the index event in Takotsubo syndrome -the ensemble achieved an accuracy of 0.864, sensitivity of 0.951, specificity of 0.763, and ROC AUC of 0.958 (Table 2; Supplementary Table S3 and Figure S3). For the full external test dataset, patient-cluster bootstrap 95% CIs were 0.866 -0.954 for accuracy and 0.938 -0.992 for ROC AUC, and weighting each patient equally yielded an accuracy of 0.903 and ROC AUC of 0.968 (Supplementary Table S4).

Human test

Seventy participants completed the reader study, including 36 cardiologists (51%); participants' characteristics were balanced across randomized groups (Supplementary Table S5). The ensemble achieved an accuracy of 0.940 on each of the two 50-ECG sets (Figure 3 and Table 3).

ECG-set composition is shown in Supplementary Table S6.

In Phases 1 -3, the GLMM-estimated probability of correct diagnosis was 0.632 (95% CI, In the randomized Phase 4, 22, 23, and 25 participants were assigned to the reference sheet-only, AI-without-Grad-CAM, and AI-with-Grad-CAM groups, respectively. The model-estimated probability of correct diagnosis was 0.608 (0.513 -0.695), 0.844 (0.784 -0.889), and 0.890 (0.845 -0.923), respectively. In the prespecified primary comparison, participants with AI assistance had significantly higher accuracy than those with the reference sheet alone (OR 4.28, 95% CI 3.08 -5.95, P < 0.001). In the prespecified secondary comparison, AI assistance with Grad-CAM was superior to AI assistance without Grad-CAM (OR 1.53, 1.04 -2.25, P = 0.033).

Additional pairwise comparisons among the three Phase 4 groups are shown in Supplementary Figure S4. Participant-level analyses were concordant (Kruskal-Wallis P < 0.001; AI vs no AI, P < 0.001; Grad-CAM vs no-Grad-CAM, P = 0.027) (Figure 3B). Stratification-adjusted estimates were similar (Supplementary Table S7).

In exploratory analyses of Phases 1 -3, the benefit of AI assistance differed by participants' experience. Interaction terms were significant for phase x experience group, phase x years of experience, and phase x cardiologist status (all P < 0.001). In Phase 1, cardiologists were more accurate than non-cardiologists (OR 1.76, 95% CI 1.44 -2.14, P < 0.001). With AI assistance (Phase 3), less experienced participants achieved higher accuracy than more experienced participants (≥ 10 vs < 10 years: OR 0.59, 95% CI 0.45 -0.76, P < 0.001), and non-cardiologists outperformed cardiologists (cardiologists vs non-cardiologists: OR 0.67, 95% CI 0.53 -0.85, P < 0.001). Thus, although all groups improved with AI assistance, the incremental benefit was greatest among less experienced participants (Figure 4A; Supplementary Figures S5 and S6). Reader-level changes in observed accuracy are shown in Figure 4B-D.

Discussion

In this study, we developed an ECG-based deep learning model to differentiate Takotsubo syndrome from acute anterior STEMI, including patients with or without coronary recanalization. Through self-supervised pretraining and integration of transformer-based, convolutional neural network-based, and multilayer perceptron-based models, the ensemble achieved robust diagnostic performance on an external validation dataset and exceeded mean reader accuracy on the reader-study ECG sets. In the reader study, AI assistance improved diagnostic accuracy, and adding Grad-CAM visualization provided further benefit. Exploratory analyses suggested that both improvement and AI-assisted accuracy varied by cardiologist status and clinical experience. These findings support prospective evaluation of AI-assisted ECG interpretation as an adjunct to clinical assessment. Its clinical impact, however, may depend on how effectively it is incorporated into human decision-making.

Several prior studies have addressed the differentiation of Takotsubo syndrome from acute myocardial infarction using ECG-based criteria or machine learning approaches. [16] [17] [18] [19] 21, 22 Shimizu et al. showed that machine learning using automatically extracted ECG parameters could distinguish Takotsubo syndrome from anterior myocardial infarction, and Hakansson et al.

subsequently demonstrated in a larger registry-based cohort that an ECG-based machine learning model outperformed cardiologists using conventional ECG criteria. However, these studies focused mainly on model performance and did not fully address input usability, temporal ECG changes after symptom onset, or the effect of AI assistance on human diagnostic performance. Models requiring structured signal data or device-specific automated measurements may be difficult to apply across manufacturers or to ECGs archived or shared as images. By treating ECGs as images, our approach may be applicable to real-world formats, including photographs of paper-or display-based ECGs. The use of ECGs obtained within two weeks of symptom onset in both Takotsubo syndrome and anterior AMI, including post-reperfusion AMI patients, captured temporal changes encountered in routine clinical practice and allowed inclusion of patients with delayed presentation. Our human diagnostic test showed that AI assistance improved accuracy beyond unaided interpretation and conventional criteria-based support, while also revealing a potential utilization gap according to clinical experience.

Without TTA, self-supervised pretraining improved individual-model ROC AUCs by 0.17 -0.23 relative to training from scratch. The final ensemble improved AUC by 0.007 -0.019 over its components. It also had the highest observed AUC among the four ensemble configurations evaluated (Supplementary Table S2), supporting a potential benefit of architectural diversity for this task. Clinicians interpret ECGs by integrating local waveform morphology, inter-lead relationships, and overall spatial patterns. ConvNeXtV2's convolutional inductive bias favors local morphological feature extraction, whereas ViT's global self-attention supports modeling of long-range spatial relationships, potentially including relationships across ECG leads. 23, 24 MLP-Mixer integrates information across image patches and feature channels. 25 These differences in inductive biases and information processing may provide complementary representations of ECG patterns, although the features underlying the ensemble gain were not directly assessed. TTA further improved discrimination for ViT and MLP-Mixer (Supplementary Table S1). These findings are consistent with prior work supporting ensembles for complex classification tasks. 26, 27 Overlapping ECG findings and temporal changes, including after spontaneous recanalization or coronary revascularization, complicate differentiation. Echocardiography may also be limited by suboptimal image acquisition, restricted patient positioning, and anatomical variations such as a wrapped LAD. These challenges support evaluating ECG models across the first two weeks after symptom onset, including after reperfusion. Performance was largely maintained when only each patient's first recorded ECG was analyzed (ROC AUC 0.958), which corresponds to the initial diagnostic decision before coronary angiography. One possible explanation for the superior performance of the AI model relative to human readers supported by criteria derived from prior reports is that AI models can capture subtle and complex ECG image patterns that are difficult to formalize as explicit diagnostic criteria.

Traditional approaches rely on predefined features, such as ST-segment elevation/depression and T-wave inversion, as well as heuristic rules, which may not fully account for the variability observed in real-world ECGs. In contrast, deep neural network models can learn high-dimensional representations directly from ECG image data, enabling them to detect patterns that may not be readily apparent to human observers. However, high predictive performance alone does not guarantee clinical impact; AI models must also be adopted and effectively used in real-world practice. Although many prior studies have demonstrated impressive model performance under experimental conditions, the integration of AI into clinical workflows and its influence on human decision-making remain insufficiently explored. Our human diagnostic test, which included 70 participants, represents one of the larger ECG reader studies evaluating AI-assisted interpretation and provides clinically relevant insights into AI-human interaction across a broad range of clinical experience. AI assistance significantly improved diagnostic accuracy compared with both unaided interpretation and conventional criteria-based support using a reference sheet derived from prior reports. Notably, randomized Phase 4 directly evaluated the incremental value of visual explanations: adding Grad-CAM heatmaps improved reader accuracy compared with AI assistance without these visualizations. This finding represents one of the relatively few examples in which an explainability component was associated with a measurable improvement in human diagnostic performance. These visual explanations may have encouraged participants to more readily incorporate AI predictions into their decisions, enhanced their own diagnostic interpretation, or both. However, even with AI assistance, mean reader accuracy remained below that of the AI model alone (0.94); only one reader exceeded the model's accuracy, achieving 0.96 in Phase 4. This finding underscores that the existence of a high-performance model does not necessarily ensure its full utilization by human users. Further studies are needed to determine how the usability, interpretability, and implementation of AI-based decision-support tools, including their user interface and user experience, can be optimized to facilitate the appropriate use of AI predictions in clinical decision-making.

A similar pattern of smaller gains and lower AI-assisted accuracy among more experienced readers was observed in our previous study of AI-assisted prediction of elevated B-type natriuretic peptide levels from chest radiographs. 8 Taken together with the superior performance of the model itself, this finding may be regarded as one form of progress in medical AI, yet it also carries a certain bittersweet irony. In this narrowly defined task, a data-driven model outperformed familiar human-centered approaches grounded in prior ECG studies and accumulated clinical experience. This echoes Sutton's "Bitter Lesson": throughout the history of AI research, major breakthroughs have often emerged not from encoding ever more human knowledge into systems, but from general methods capable of exploiting increasing computational resources. 28 Across our two reader studies, this paradoxical pattern was characterized by three features: (1) AI assistance improved accuracy in both more and less experienced groups, with larger gains among less experienced readers; (2) the unaided advantage associated with cardiologist status in the present study and greater clinical experience in our previous study reversed with AI assistance; and (3) mean AI-assisted reader accuracy nevertheless remained below that of the AI model alone. 8 Taken together, these findings suggest that conventional clinical expertise does not necessarily translate into effective use of AI assistance and that the relative performance of reader groups can reverse under AI support. These observations motivate a utilization bias hypothesis: greater reliance on judgements grounded in prior expertise may limit the effective incorporation of AI predictions, contributing to smaller performance gains and lower AI-assisted accuracy. This paradoxical pattern among more experienced readers may reflect a tendency to place greater weight on their own judgements than on AI predictions, potentially consistent with anchoring on prior judgements or cognitive entrenchment associated with expertise. These mechanisms were not directly assessed. The paradoxical pattern observed in our study may arise not only in AI-assisted decision-making but also in other fields, reflecting a possible tension between established expertise and adaptation to new tools or approaches.

Although individual AI models may be rapidly superseded by more capable alternatives, challenges in their effective use, including the proposed utilization bias, may persist across successive generations of tools. At the same time, overreliance on AI assistance may impair unaided performance when users are subsequently required to complete similar tasks without AI support. 29 Although AI tools can improve performance on narrowly defined tasks that are amenable to a divide-and-conquer approach, overreliance on them may contribute to the erosion of human skills. Traditional learning and human-centered approaches may help mitigate this risk. Even if more powerful AI agents or artificial general intelligence-like systems eventually emerge as potential "silver bullets," a reasonable equilibrium may lie in combining effective use of such tools with continued human-centered training that encourages openness to new approaches, while pursuing sustained, incremental improvements in how the two are integrated.

Study limitations

Several limitations should be considered. This was a retrospective study, and although an external validation dataset was included, further prospective validation is required to assess generalizability. The datasets were derived from specific institutions, and differences in patient populations, ECG acquisition methods, and clinical practice patterns may limit broader applicability. Performance was evaluated at the image level with multiple images per patient; however, patient-cluster bootstrap CIs and equal weighting of patients yielded similar estimates.

The analysis was based on ECG data alone and did not incorporate clinical information, laboratory data, or imaging findings. Incorporating such information may further improve diagnostic performance. The human diagnostic test was conducted under controlled conditions and may not fully reflect real-world clinical workflows. Repeated ECG exposure in Phases 1 -3 and patients shared between the two sets may have introduced learning or recall effects. Phase 4 randomization was expected to balance residual learning effects across groups. Finally, participants' trust in AI and their reasons for accepting or rejecting AI predictions were not directly assessed.

Conclusions

We developed an ECG image-based ensemble deep learning model that accurately differentiated Takotsubo syndrome from acute anterior STEMI. AI assistance improved reader accuracy, with additional benefit from visual explanations. Exploratory analyses suggested that both performance gains and AI-assisted accuracy varied by reader background and clinical experience. AI-supported ECG interpretation may enhance clinical decision-making in challenging diagnostic scenarios; further studies should evaluate performance in real-world clinical practice and address potential utilization gaps. AI, artificial intelligence; AUC, area under the receiver operating characteristic curve; CI, confidence interval; ECG, electrocardiogram; Grad-CAM, gradient-weighted class activation mapping; MLP, multilayer perceptron; OR, odds ratio; STEMI, ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction; ViT, Vision Transformer. Alt text: Three panels show ECG datasets from two hospitals, a three-model ensemble distinguishing Takotsubo syndrome from acute anterior STEMI (external validation AUC 0.970), and results for 70 readers. In randomized Phase 4, the estimated probability of correct diagnosis was 61% with a reference sheet alone, 84% with added AI predictions, and 89% with predictions plus heatmaps. In exploratory analyses, cardiologists' unaided advantage reversed with AI assistance. This study comprised three stages: (A) Development and external-validation ECG image datasets were collected from two hospitals; (B) three self-supervised pretrained backbone models were fine-tuned and combined into the final ensemble model, which was evaluated on the external test dataset; and (C) the final model was tested against human readers in a 4-phase diagnostic study. (A) For self-supervised pretraining, 1,754,276 ECG images were generated from four publicly available databases (MIMIC-IV-ECG, A Large Scale 12-Lead Electrocardiogram Database for Arrhythmia Study, PTB-XL, and Georgia 12-Lead ECG Challenge Dataset). Additional institutional ECG images (Hiroshima City North Medical Center Asa Citizens Hospital) were used for pretraining and pretraining validation. Development and external-validation datasets were collected from two hospitals. Patients with acute anterior STEMI (left main or left anterior descending culprit) or Takotsubo syndrome, and healthy volunteers, contributed 12-lead ECGs; those recorded within 14 days after symptom onset were labeled AMI or Takotsubo, whereas those recorded before onset or at least 1 year after onset were labeled non-Takotsubo/AMI. The development dataset (832 participants) was divided at the participant level into training (n = 633; 76%), validation (n = 149; 18%), and pretraining-validation (n = 50; 6%) sets. (B) Three backbone models (Vision Transformer, ConvNeXtV2, and MLP-Mixer) were pretrained on unlabeled ECG images by MAE-based self-supervised learning, in which randomly masked image patches are reconstructed so that the models learn general ECG image representations without diagnostic labels. Each model was then fine-tuned for 3-class and subsequently binary classification, with TTA applied where ROC curves (A) and PR curves (B) of the final ensemble model and its three component models for predicting AMI. The calibration plot (C) shows the agreement between predicted probability and observed proportion of AMI for the final ensemble model. The confusion matrix (D) shows image-based classification by the final ensemble model at a predicted AMI probability threshold of 0.5. Each cell shows the number of ECG images and the corresponding row percentage, calculated using the total number of images in each true diagnostic class as the (A) Exploratory within-phase association of cardiologist status and clinical experience with diagnostic correctness in Phases 1 and 3. Squares indicate ORs and horizontal lines indicate 95% CIs; the dashed line indicates an OR of 1. ORs, 95% CIs, and P values were obtained from generalized linear mixed models with random intercepts for readers and ECG cases. (B -D) Reader-level changes in observed accuracy from Phase 1 to Phase 2 (B), Phase 1 to Phase 3 (C), and Phase 2 to Phase 3 (D). Each bar represents one reader's paired change in accuracy, expressed in percentage points; positive values indicate higher accuracy in the later phase. Orange and blue indicate cardiologists and non-cardiologists, respectively. Dots on the zero line indicate unchanged accuracy. Readers are sorted independently in descending order within each panel; the same horizontal position therefore does not necessarily represent the same reader across panels. AI indicates artificial intelligence; CI, confidence interval; ECG, electrocardiogram; and OR, odds ratio. Alt text: A forest plot shows exploratory association of cardiologist status and experience with correctness in Phases 1 and 3. Three waterfall plots show each reader's change in accuracy for Phase 2 minus Phase 1, Phase 3 minus Phase 1, and Phase 3 minus Phase 2, with cardiologist status indicated by colour. Table 3. Supplementary Methods

Supplementary Tables S1 -S8 Supplementary Figures S1 -S6 TRIPOD+AI checklist STARD-AI checklist

Diagnostic correctness at the ECG-response level was the primary outcome and was analyzed using binomial generalized linear mixed-effects models (GLMMs) with a logit link and random intercepts for participants and ECG cases. Fixed effects were phase for Phases 1 -3 and randomized group for Phase 4. Prespecified contrasts compared Phase 2 vs Phase 1, Phase 3 vs Phase 2, and Phase 3 vs Phase 1. For all three pairwise comparisons among Phases 1 -3, P 0.582 -0.678) in Phase 1 (unaided), 0.608 (0.558 -0.656) in Phase 2 (reference sheet), and 0.864 (0.837 -0.888) in Phase 3 (reference sheet + AI).

The accuracy in Phase 3 was significantly higher than in Phase 1 (odds ratio (OR) 3.71; 95% CI, 3.21 -4.29, P < 0.001) and in Phase 2 (OR 4.11, 3.55 -4.75, P < 0.001), whereas the reference sheet alone slightly reduced accuracy relative to unaided interpretation (Phase 2 vs Phase 1, OR 0.90, 0.80 -1.02, P = 0.049). The participant-level analysis was concordant (Friedman P < 0.001, Phase 3 vs Phase 1 and Phase 3 vs Phase 2, both P < 0.001) (Figure 3). At the reader level, accuracy increased from Phase 1 to Phase 3 in 68 of 70 readers and from Phase 2 to Phase 3 in 69 readers, whereas it decreased from Phase 1 to Phase 2 in 37 readers (Figure 4).
